This rule usually protects women who do not pay a lawyer or the husband's lawyer. Court with you. Dr. Böhmdorfer also submitted a similar proposal for Austria and immediately reaped criticisms in a reflective manner. In my opinion, this criticism was inaccurate and preliminary, because we are very often faced with the fact that unmistakable women enter the divorce in agreement, make concessions, and only much later recognize the significance of it. That is why, because professional representation has been politically rejected, we have now included in this law an obligation to inform the judge. He is supposed to take a picture of the knowledge of the incessant partner. It should then draw this partner's attention to the divorce consequences, in particular to social security consequences, credit consequences, but also to what it means to refrain from maintenance. The judge's obligation to inform is a standard of protection, which we particularly welcome. ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e10643	Mr President. Minister! High house! I would like to follow Mr Partik-Pablé's statement and say that I also think that this international regulation is very important, and that we are creating new rules in terms of evidence. [[Abg. Dr. Puswald: I wouldn't!]] In the rule of law, in civil proceedings and in the rules on travel fees, we regulate, in accordance with a regulation of the EU, namely Regulation 1206/2001, exactly: of 28. May 2001, those measures that allow foreign courts to include evidence here in Austria – or vice versa, that our investigators in the EU Member States can make evidence: beyond the instrument of legal assistance known to and for themselves. In this context, we are extending this work not only to EU countries, but also – under very strict approval criteria – to other countries. Foreign courts can then work with us if there is first reciprocity; that is, if we are allowed to act with them, they may also be with us. Secondly, if it does not violate any fundamental rights, that is, our rule of law we shall not be affected. And thirdly, if it does not involve any forced measures, such as house searches or arrest warrants. And fourthly, the Austrian judge can of course be present if surveys are conducted directly within the framework of the investigations. In particular, we are interested in reciprocity because our courts also occasionally want to receive evidence abroad. With this novel – that has already been mentioned by colleague Partik-Pablé – we give up part of our sovereignty if we allow foreign courts to act with us. However, we do not give up our basic principles of the rule of law. I therefore believe that this principle must also be maintained for the future, namely: international cooperation – but not at the expense of our rule of law. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e10918	Mr President, thank you for your confidence. Dear High House! Mr. Federal Minister! I would first like to make a brief statement, then to colleagues Drinkl – and not to become nervous now, but to stay relaxed, colleagues from the SPÖ! –, to the Financial Security Act. We have decided, after very intensive discussions, to exclude the companies which are not professional financial market participants from the regulation of this law. However, I would like to point out that we are also demonstrating the great confidence that we all have in the Justice Ministers – we have also made a committee statement: The Justice Committee assumes “that the Federal Ministry of Justice is continuing to observe the situation”, which is happening for small and medium-sized enterprises in particular, and that “in the event of a negative impact” of our derogation “proposals for a corresponding change in the scope of application”. Why that? . We believe that it is justified at the moment in a consideration of goods and is objectively correct to make this exception for small and medium-sized enterprises. However, we also know that European development is now only under way and that the EU countries, which are often also the important Austrian trading partners, tend to make this regulation without exceptions. It will be important to see how the scheme will finally look in Germany. We cannot, therefore, rule out the fact that the regulation we are now considering should, as a consequence, prove to be a disadvantage for small and medium-sized enterprises in particular. ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e12423	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! High house! I would like to comment briefly on the foreign exchange law. The foreign exchange law from 1946 – colleague Fassleven has already said it – was marked by a completely different being and spirit than that of today's European and euro-reality, and the adaptation we are making together was therefore excessive and necessary. It should not be forgotten that a law which is characterised by such restrictions and controls also entails a huge additional effort both in public administration and in the companies concerned. And effort means cost in particular. It is therefore also to be welcomed from this point of view that this adjustment can now be made. I would, however, like to highlight one aspect, namely a consumer protection aspect. At the same time as the foreign exchange law, we have adopted the Transfer Act, in accordance with the Regulation of the European Parliament and of the Council of 19 March 1990 on the free movement of goods and services. In December 2001, it is established that cross-border intra-Community payments may not be subject to higher charges than they have so far been for domestic payment transfers. We are now implementing this with the new § 7a. But above all, we also stand up to the relevant information and information obligations, which are imposed on monetary institutions which not only have to show fees or fees, but now also need to clarify the corresponding exchange fees when buying and selling euro, but in particular – and this is due to the effectiveness of the low fees in the case of transfers – customers must also be informed about the international account number such as the international bank code, which is necessary to enable them to use this discounted cost structure for themselves, in particular for the recipient. All in all, then, a law that not only adapts to current market conditions, but also, in particular, to consumers, means a substantial improvement and a reduction in costs. I am glad that we will be able to decide unanimously today.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e12851	Mr President. High house! I will now try to open up all the secrets of the supposedly secret reform. I have already tried to explain this to the Financial Committee, but I am now working on all the issues open individually. Purpose of tobacco tax. Mr Kogler, there is no commitment to tobacco tax. As you have rightly said, it is not appropriate for financial reasons, for financial reasons, to establish a commitment of purpose, because it creates an inflexible budget management. It is possible to define priorities on a continuous basis without commitment. But we have more revenue through a higher tobacco tax. We have therefore reached an agreement with the Minister of Social Affairs in the course of the design of the budget for 2003 and 2004 on how to use these more revenue in budgetary terms. But this applies first to the 2003 and 2004 budgets. To the tax administration. Why, at all, a reform of the entire tax administration? – The aim is to speed up the handling of tax returns. If you make a tax declaration today as an employee over the Internet, you will normally have this declaration of tax the next day via the Internet. – The previous manual procedure took weeks. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Second objective: Better service, especially for payroll taxpayers and SMEs, all those persons and businesses that cannot afford expensive tax advisors. In the context of the tax administration reform, no financial office is closed. It is very important for regional specificities: no financial office is closed! So I'm gonna give you a location guarantee here. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Each financial office shall set up an information centre after the same design, where the financial officer shall provide the citizen with care and advice. He can also issue his tax declaration there and also receives help there. I think that's a very important function. [[Abg. May. Gaßner: An entrance site!]] – No, not a point of entry, but a point of advice. I'll invite you. I'll show you how this looks and works. This is not a normal place of entry, but, as has been said, an advisory body. Further objective is: We want even examination intervals for Austria as a whole – and we do. So far, small financial offices in rural regions have regularly carried out an audit every three years, because they have had sufficient staff, others in agglomerations have had ten-year examination intervals. That's not tax justice! It is therefore the aim of all establishments to be checked at even intervals. Another important project is to intensify the fight against corruption, particularly in the field of VAT. How do we want to achieve this? – First of all – and this is the first point why we need this change today – we have removed the so-called vocational Senate from the financial country directors and have made it an independent financial Senate, that is, a kind of financial court, where, unlike earlier, there is a similar position, i.e. equal opportunities between the tax client and the financial office. These must occur together with the same accounts and obligations before the financial judge. In addition, all decisions will be published – and not so often as before. This is intended to serve a uniform execution, including in the appeal procedure, and to make the entire procedure transparent. The next measure was – and it starts with 1. January 2004 – that the 80 financial offices are allocated across Austria to 43 economic areas, i.e. two or three financial offices are under a single leadership. One is responsible for overall management, and it also has a second from the so-called subject area, which advises tax and pays attention to a uniform execution. This economic area has the advantage that there will be a balance between two or three financial offices and that the same examination intervals can be observed more easily. It is a concept with which to decentralize. Highly specialized professionals come from the financial country directors to the regions, i.e. the regions are not taken away from high-quality posts, but they are fed to them. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] The next step is to introduce a accounting agency for all public services. This is why the previous financial directorates are losing their accounts. These are brought together into a accounting agency. Therefore, for all these reasons, a reorganisation of regional management is necessary. I cannot give you any written documents on this because we are still at the last stage of this new regulation, and we have to finalise it. But the regions do not lose anything, but they have gained more staff. We also want a flattening of the entire tax organization of so far three levels – that is the concept of the 19th. This was the old administration after 1848, Freelor of the Stone – reached on two stages. Regional management will, in particular, ensure that budget and personnel management is managed in a uniform manner and, above all, respect uniform supervision of services. – That was said on this issue. Mrs Sburny, you mentioned the Investment Protection Agreement and you almost criticized the fact that there are bilateral investment protection agreements. 47 agreements are currently in force, ten agreements are signed and not yet in force, and three are initialled but not yet signed. Unfortunately, there is no multilateral investment protection agreement, so we have to resort to the old instruments of the bilateral investment protection agreements. [[Abg. May. Gaßner: Thank God!]] Our investment protection agreements are governed by international contracts that are used everywhere. It would be better if there were a multilateral, but unfortunately there is not yet. If we did not conclude such an investment protection agreement, another country would do business, and I think that cannot be done in the sense of our export economy. On tobacco tax: The new Member States have been granted a multiannual deadline for achieving the level of tobacco taxation, as it is currently with us and in other EU countries. That is why we introduced the one introduced by Minister Edlinger – not introduced by us! – Rule that only packs of up to 25 pieces can be taken can be maintained. There is no need to introduce a new authority to that effect, because after enlargement on 1 January. There is still a Schengen border on May 2004. It will therefore continue to be controlled by the passport, and in this situation it will be possible to test the compliance with this regulation in a sample manner – as it has been before. So we do not need a civilian strip, we do not need new locations, but this will continue to be controlled as a temporary measure, and over the years when the new Member States are at our level of taxation, this control will be unnecessary. That means that we no longer need them, and then there will probably be no more Schengen borders. Once again for the benefit of non-profits: This is not an economic measure, but it should strengthen companies in their own capital – this is a very special problem for us in Austria. In order not to make this a saving form, for example, we have not extended this measure to the liberals, because there are no such investment-intensive facilities in this area and therefore it is not needed. The aim is to strengthen businesses, in particular in order to be able to obtain loans under favourable conditions with regard to Basel II. I consider this system to be extremely important and favourable. Mr Bauer, you mentioned international tax harmonisation. I fully agree with you that this would be important, and that is also an issue for each ECOFIN under the item entitled ‘All-Embracing’. Everyone says it, but no one does anything. That would be a task for the EU, and as a first step we would have to come to a uniform tax base which we do not have, and then competition could only take place over the rates. That also bothers me a little bit with the model of Slovakia. With this 19 percent corporate tax, income tax there are no derogations. Even if you have a claim depreciation, you can't stop it. If you compare this with Western systems or our system, it is actually a 25 percent tax, but nevertheless this tax rate is spectacular and a challenge for us. In this respect, we must work very hard on tax reform from 2005 onwards. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e12948	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! Dear ladies and gentlemen! In my short contribution, I would like to address the sale of the shares of the federal government in the Tyrolean airport operating company at the level of 50 percent – to the same extent to the state of Tyrol and to the municipality of Innsbruck. Since the mid-1970s, the Republic of Austria has held 50 percent of the shares in this airport company, and it was very useful that in the initial phase, in the construction phase of an important infrastructure institution, money was taken over and risked to achieve success more quickly. Now the situation has changed. This co-ownership, in the sense of the original intentions of the site policy, is nothing more. It actually leads to a complication of the processes and also to a certain shift of resources. At each meeting, at every investment decision and so on, the officials of the ministry must arrive. That is to say, the Court of Auditors has long recommended that the competences should be clarified. The Federal Government has addressed this matter clearly and consistently. After Salzburg, Graz and Klagenfurt, one has now separated from the shares in the Tyrolean airport company, and what is particularly important and interesting is that a privatisation contract is also linked to this separation. In other words, the shares are not simply transferred from one public hand to the other, but the shares are actually managed by private hands in the final consequence. This has mainly to do with the commitment of regional economic operators to the anchorage of this company in the context of the regional economy. I would like to thank you very much for these measures. I think one can say that the commitment of the federal government has paid off. Today we have a thriving regional airport in Tyrol, we have a thriving regional company. It is an extremely important infrastructure for the site, which is used every week and every month by thousands of economic operators and private individuals, and which, in the end, we honourable Members can also take advantage of every week. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e22216	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! The death penalty is not a controversial subject in the political debate in Austria. There is, and we are happy about this, consensus that the death penalty is an inappropriate remedy. As with many other human rights issues, Austria has a global pioneering role here. There is no need to implement the 13th. Protocol on the Convention on the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms that the complete abolition of the death penalty is aimed at the Council of Europe in so-called war times, because the death penalty has already been abolished in Austria in 1968 thanks to the meritful work of the Klaus government. We from the ÖVP advocate a worldwide abolition of the death penalty. This is in harmony with our view of the world and our Christian attitude. In many countries it is still considered a right to kill people in order to influence the behavior of others. The death penalty is imposed in many countries, such as Iran or China, as well as in the United States of America. In 2002, 81 percent of all executions were carried out in these three countries alone. According to amnesty international figures, at least 1,060 people were executed in China. The Dun- kelziffer seems to be much higher. In Iran, at least 113 people were executed in 2002, many of them in public. And in the United States it was 71 people, there with stand of 1. January 2003 sentenced 3,700 prisoners to death. Justice errors occur again and again. In the last 20 years, 107 cases of innocent to death have been reported in the United States alone. Is the death penalty a suitable deterrent? – Against this deterrent hypothesis, a United Nations research report, which was updated in 2002. This concludes that there is no scientific evidence that the death penalty is a more dissuasive form of punishment than life imprisonment. Nor is the argument that the death penalty has a positive effect on the crime rate valid. Recent crime statistics from States that have abolished the death penalty show that the abolition in no way leads to a negative effect. In Canada, for example, after the abolition of the death penalty in 1999, the murder rate fell significantly. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! It is clear to my group and I that the death penalty is not an appropriate remedy. We therefore welcome the ratification of agreements at international level, which means that States themselves commit themselves not to apply the death penalty. We will therefore be pleased to vote in favour of this motion for a resolution.

All of Europe will thus send a sign towards those countries that still apply the death penalty. There are still 84 countries in the world where the death penalty is applied. Martin Luther King once said: “Our generation will not have so much to complain of the inactions of evil people as the terrifying silence of good.” – Europe does not keep silent, but sets a mark in the sense of humanity. Austria has been pleading for this for many years. Now it has finally succeeded to kill the right, even in times of war, once and for all.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e22597	The next speaker was Mr Dobnigg. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e22615	Mr President. Federal Minister! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) In my opinion, it is very gratifying that today in Parliament there is a 100% agreement of all parties with regard to the complete abolition of the death penalty. The protocol to be ratified today is, by the way, the first international treaty, which provides for the complete and unconditional abolition of the death penalty in both peace and war times. A court that knows the death penalty is a foreign body in any democratic system. And it has been shown that the death penalty is not more horrifying It is effective than other forms of punishment, because no statistics can document that there is a link between the death penalty and a decline in crime. Rather, in many U.S. states with death penalty, the number of murder crimes is higher than in those without them. Instead of deterrence, it's more likely to get out of the way. When the state itself kills, he shows that he approves of killing. At the same time, there is always the risk of misdemeanor. No legal system is infallible. Unfortunately, there are many examples of judicial errors in the past, but executions can no longer be reversed after misconceptions and people are unfortunately no longer made alive. Despite the most convincing arguments, it is unfortunately still necessary today to continue the political struggle against this kind of punishment. (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) High house! It is therefore also one of the most important and urgent tasks of the European Union, on the one hand, and of the members of our Austrian Federal Government, on the other hand, in particular of the Minister of Justice and the Foreign Minister, to make greater pressure on discussions and negotiations so that this inhumane form of punishment is finally abolished worldwide.

It should no longer be possible to go over the population, about interests, about social demands and about important concerns, for example of Austrian minorities. - Thank you.

Point First reading: Request by Mr Gabriele Heinisch-Hosek, Members of Parliament, concerning a federal law amending the Federal Law on the Rules of Procedure of the National Council (155/A)
ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e23916	Finally, we arrive at the 26th. Agenda item. We are going into the debate. I call Mrs Heinisch-Hosek. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e23940	Mr President. High house! Very soon: This request calls for an own Committee on Children and Youth. The current Rules of Procedure provide for the assignment of a proposal to a committee, consult the technical committee, and then find and vote on it. The idea here is a novum. The situation is as follows: We believe that an own Committee on Children and Youth should be able to decide to draw up proposals from the other specialist committees for which we want to deliver opinions, i.e. to set up its own agenda itself, to be able to draw back the opinions to the committees and, of course, to hope that they can be included in the deliberations. [[President Dr. Khol will again take over the presidency.]] This would be a further step in that children's and youth policies are not only treated together with family policy. We know that child and youth policy is a cross-sectional matter, almost every committee has an issue. An own Committee on Children and Youth in this form could ensure that we as our own advisory committee, supported by experts, return our knowledge to the specialist committees. I hope you can discuss this positively. - Thank you.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e23978	Mr Pack is now speaking to us for 2 minutes. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e23997	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! In short, referring to the reasons for your request: It is true that the situation, emancipation and participation of children and young people in all their social areas are crucial factors for the development and future of our democracy. It is also almost right that many legal provisions concerning children and young people are currently being discussed in the various national council committees, but only almost right, because in fact all legal provisions apply sooner or later also to children and young people. In order to increase and strengthen the importance of child and youth-related issues, you need to set up your own Committee for Children and Youth. That is what we think is quite wrong, because this demand is clearly going in the wrong direction. Why? – Because, first of all, the installation of a separate Committee on Children’s and Youth’s Affairs, as you plan, is not in accordance with the Rules of Procedure, and secondly, I believe that this request has the appearance of an Alibi action. You create a body of your own, in which you can then simply let your concerns disappear. It is clear that setting up only formal criteria is too little. Rather, it is crucial that children and young people are actually implemented, i.e. that effective representation and implementation is given in all committees, not only in a pro-forma children's and youth committee. An efficient representation and implementation, which is a special concern of the Austrian People's Party. At the moment, children and young people are very well removed in the Family Committee. This was also demonstrated by the last Committee meeting, which discussed the report on the situation of young people in detail for at least four hours and then discussed two motions for resolutions on youth. Thank you.

We are always open to young people's problems and can say: At the moment, we are doing a good job for young people in the Family Committee and also in the government. ParlaMint-AT_2003-11-13-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00038_d7e24116	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! Firstly, I would like to point out that we are of the opinion shared by the intention of this request. Whether this is the corresponding form and business order, that is another question. But if I look at the comments made by Mr Lichtenegger, who explained to us how great the Family Committee works and that the interests of young people are represented there anyway, I think that we should look at them more precisely where the differences lie, because that is exactly the problem. If there was really a discussion on which one felt that the concerns of children and young people were being felt, and if, for example, the business opportunities, such as the invitation of informers, were to be perceived, then such requests would not be required – so to speak, as an aid measure for the opposition to provide such support here. I believe that we should deal with this proposal for a Rules of Procedure in a serious way and at least look at the possibilities that exist, even if you say that there is sufficient legal standardisation, to better anchor the interests of children and young people in Parliament.
